        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_6/projects/faith-evidence-ie/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires reasoning across sentence boundaries to identify relations between entity pairs.\nDocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} established the first large-scale benchmark for this task, catalyzing a line of encoder-based methods that treat DocRE as classification over entity pair representations.\nATLOP \\citep{zhou2021atlop} introduced adaptive thresholding for multi-label prediction and localized context pooling for entity representations.\nA parallel thread incorporates sentence-level evidence into these architectures.\nSAIS \\citep{xiao2022sais} introduced evidence supervision as an auxiliary training signal and showed that joint evidence prediction improves extraction through data augmentation and ensemble inference.\nDREEAM \\citep{ma2023dreeam} extended this approach with dynamic memory-based evidence retrieval that guides attention to supporting sentences.\nEIDER \\citep{xie2022eider} jointly trains an evidence extractor and fuses evidence representations into the relation classifier.\nIn all three systems, evidence identification is binary sentence classification over a fixed candidate set, a formulation that adds no output complexity.\nRecent work has recast DocRE for large language models.\nAutoRE \\citep{autore2024} fine-tunes LLMs with QLoRA using a relation-head-facts decomposition; \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame extraction as multi-choice reading comprehension; RelPrior \\citep{wang2025relprior} uses relation priors to guide LLM-based extraction; GLiDRE \\citep{zhang2025glidre} presents a generalist lightweight approach; Ru-LLM \\citep{wang2025rullm} incorporates logical rule constraints; and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods match or surpass encoder baselines but produce bare triplets without evidence.\nThe critical distinction lies in computational nature: in SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is classification that regularizes the encoder without increasing output length; in LLM-based DocRE, evidence requires additional output tokens per triple, imposing costs that depend on model capacity and optimization strategy.\nWhether this cost is inevitable or can be mitigated is the central question of our work.\n\nGroup Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}) eliminates the separate value network by computing advantages within groups.\nSubsequent work addresses failure modes: DAPO \\citep{yu2025dapo} prevents entropy collapse, Dr.\\ GRPO \\citep{liu2025drgrpo} corrects length bias, and multi-objective variants \\citep{liu2026gdpo,ito2025mogrpo} handle competing reward signals.\nApplications to structured IE include R1-RE \\citep{dai2025r1re} and REAR \\citep{wang2025rear}.\nDirect Preference Optimization \\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF as implicit reward maximization.\nOur decoupling spectrum is distinct from catastrophic forgetting in RL alignment \\citep{jin2025rl_panacea}, where models lose pre-trained capabilities across tasks; in our setting, extraction and evidence compete \\emph{within} a single task, resembling negative transfer in multi-task learning \\citep{ruder2017overview,standley2020tasks,yu2020gradient}, amplified by QLoRA's constrained parameter space \\citep{gao2023scaling,moskovitz2024constrained}.\nRSFT avoids this intra-task competition by operating on discrete outputs rather than gradients, extending the sample-and-select principle \\citep{wang2023selfconsistency} to structured IE with joint quality scoring.\n\nEvidence faithfulness evaluation has received increasing attention: \\citet{li2025drift} propose dual-reward inference for faithful rationale generation, and \\citet{zhang2024infuse} introduce fine-grained NLI-based faithfulness metrics for summarization.\nOur CED scoring builds on similar NLI-based principles but targets evidence-relation entailment rather than free-form rationales.\n\n\\citet{zelikman2022star} introduced STaR for iterative self-improvement; \\citet{dong2023raft} generalize this with reward-model-based sample filtering; recent analysis shows rejection sampling implicitly performs PPO-like optimization \\citep{tang2025rsftppo}.\nOur RSFT is a single-round variant applied to evidence-grounded DocRE with CED/NLI scoring as a domain-specific quality filter.\nBecause RSFT selects complete outputs rather than differentiating through a reward signal, it cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods discover, inherently preserving joint quality.\n",
        "numLines": 31,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 31
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex"
  },

{
  "parentUuid": "a339bff9-47a1-48f7-b975-cb63bce1273b",

  "attachment": {

    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_6/projects/faith-evidence-ie/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_6/projects/faith-evidence-ie/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset.}\nWe conduct experiments on DocRED~\\citep{yao2019docred}, a large-scale document-level relation extraction benchmark containing 3,053 training and 998 development documents spanning 96 relation types. Each document requires identifying relational triples across multiple sentences and grounding each triple with supporting evidence sentences. We use Re-DocRED~\\citep{tan2022redocred} for cross-dataset generalization evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Models.}\nOur primary model is Qwen3-4B. To study scale effects, we additionally train Qwen3-1.7B and Qwen3-8B under identical configurations. We include LLaMA-3.1-8B as a cross-family validation point.\n\n\\paragraph{Training.}\nAll models use LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} with rank $r{=}64$ and $\\alpha{=}128$ over a structured output format that jointly produces relation triples and evidence sentences. For RSFT, we sample $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per document and select the top-$k{=}1$ response ranked by CED scoring (\\S\\ref{sec:ced}), which combines NLI-based evidence faithfulness with extraction accuracy. GRPO experiments use group sizes $G{=}4$ (QLoRA, 4-bit) and $G{=}8$ (bf16 full-precision LoRA). DPO constructs preference pairs from CED-ranked outputs.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics.}\n\\textbf{Ign-F1} (primary) ignores training-set triples per the official DocRED protocol. \\textbf{Rel-F1} measures extraction F1 on all triples. \\textbf{Evi-F1 (TP-only)} measures evidence F1 conditioned on correct triples; \\textbf{Evi-F1 (joint)} follows the DREEAM protocol~\\citep{ma2023dreeam} for cross-method comparison. \\textbf{EDCR} (\\S\\ref{sec:edcr}) diagnoses extraction-evidence decoupling. All results use the development set.\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison on Qwen3-4B. The SFT baseline exhibits high seed sensitivity (mean Ign-F1 43.58, std 3.80pp, range 39.74--47.34). Removing evidence entirely (no-evidence SFT, s42) reaches 47.21 Ign-F1.\n\nRSFT CED achieves 44.82 Ign-F1 (+1.24 over SFT mean) with substantially lower variance (std 0.51pp), though these reflect different sources of variation (Section~\\ref{sec:scaling}). Even the weakest RSFT seed (44.23) exceeds the SFT mean. Evidence quality improves (Evi-F1 80.96 vs.\\ 79.61) and coupling tightens (EDCR 0.6487 vs.\\ 0.6797). Per-seed results appear in Appendix~\\ref{sec:ablation_full}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Main results on DocRED dev (Qwen3-4B). Ign-F1 shows mean $\\pm$ std$^{(\\text{seeds})}$; other metrics are same-seed means. Best \\textbf{bold}; best evidence-generating \\underline{underlined}. Per-seed: Appendix~\\ref{tab:ablation_full}.}\n\\label{tab:main}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Ign-F1} & \\textbf{Rel-F1} & \\textbf{Evi-F1} & \\textbf{EDCR} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSFT baseline & 43.58{\\scriptsize$\\pm$3.80}$^{(3)}$ & 44.39 & 79.61 & 0.6797 \\\\\nSFT no-evidence & \\textbf{47.21} & \\textbf{48.76} & -- & -- \\\\\nRSFT CED & \\underline{44.82{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.51}$^{(3)}$} & \\underline{45.80} & 80.96 & 0.6487 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Evidence-Extraction Decoupling Spectrum}\n\\label{sec:spectrum}\n\nDifferent RL algorithms produce qualitatively different failure modes when applied to joint evidence-and-extraction generation. We organize these outcomes along a \\emph{decoupling spectrum} ranging from catastrophic collapse to mild perturbation, shown in Table~\\ref{tab:spectrum} (visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:spectrum}, Appendix).\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO QLoRA: catastrophic collapse.}\nUnder 4-bit quantization ($G{=}4$), GRPO drives Rel-F1 to 1.38 while Evi-F1 rises to 85.04 (EDCR 0.9876): the model produces well-formed evidence but loses extraction capability. This extreme decoupling co-occurs with constrained precision; full-precision GRPO (below) avoids it.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO bf16: mild, variable.}\nFull-precision GRPO ($G{=}8$) avoids catastrophe, with the best checkpoint achieving Rel-F1 45.49, Evi-F1 80.86, and EDCR 0.6318 (below SFT baseline 0.6797). Both sub-tasks improve jointly, though the variable training trajectory requires careful checkpoint selection.\n\n\\paragraph{DPO: gradual decay.}\nDPO progressively trades extraction for evidence: Rel-F1 drops from 40.34 (1 epoch) to 34.29 (3 epochs)\\footnote{DPO 3-epoch results use an earlier evaluation protocol; included for trend analysis.} while evidence improves (Evi-F1 80.88$\\to$84.03, EDCR 0.7139$\\to$0.7294).\n\n\\paragraph{RSFT: no decoupling.}\nRSFT maintains both capabilities simultaneously (Rel-F1 45.80, Evi-F1 80.96, EDCR 0.6487) because discrete output selection cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Evidence-extraction decoupling across RL paradigms (Qwen3-4B). Severity indicates the degree of extraction collapse relative to evidence improvement.}\n\\label{tab:spectrum}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Paradigm} & \\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Rel-F1} & \\textbf{Evi-F1} & \\textbf{EDCR} & \\textbf{Severity} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGRPO & QLoRA $G{=}4$ & 1.38 & 85.04 & 0.9876 & Catastrophic \\\\\nGRPO & bf16 $G{=}8$ & 45.49 & 80.86 & 0.6318 & Mild \\\\\nDPO & bf16 1ep & 40.34 & 80.88 & 0.7139 & Gradual \\\\\nDPO & bf16 3ep$^\\dagger$ & 34.29 & 84.03 & 0.7294 & Gradual \\\\\n\\midrule\nSFT & baseline$^\\ddagger$ & 44.39 & 79.61 & 0.6797 & None \\\\\nRSFT & CED$^\\ddagger$ & 45.80 & 80.96 & 0.6487 & None \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Earlier evaluation protocol. $^\\ddagger$Three-seed mean. Severity reflects training trajectory stability (propensity for extraction collapse during optimization), not the final-checkpoint EDCR value.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Scale-Dependent Evidence Tax}\n\\label{sec:scaling}\n\nThe evidence tax, defined as Ign-F1(no-evidence) $-$ Ign-F1(with-evidence) on the development set, is scale-dependent. Under matched evaluation conditions (\\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}$=$2048, format-compliant intersection; Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_tax}), the training-time tax reveals a within-family capacity threshold: evidence generation benefits 1.7B ($-$5.63pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_tax}), possibly through implicit regularization that forces attention to supporting context, but is approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B, $n{=}3$; +0.04pp at 8B; +0.03pp at 14B). Table~\\ref{tab:scaling} presents RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across five models (Figure~\\ref{fig:scaling} in Appendix).\n\nRSFT navigates this capacity landscape through quality-based data selection rather than gradient pressure. Mean Ign-F1 improves across tested scales: +6.55pp at 1.7B, +1.24pp at 4B, +4.33pp at 8B, and +3.79pp at 14B (Table~\\ref{tab:scaling}). RSFT from an independent SFT base (s44, Method B) achieves 51.52 Ign-F1 at 4B (+4.18pp over that base). This result confirms that RSFT gains are not contingent on a specific SFT checkpoint.\n\nBeyond mean performance, RSFT substantially improves training reliability. Three RSFT seeds from a shared SFT checkpoint yield standard deviations of 0.09pp at 1.7B, 0.51pp at 4B, and 0.19pp at 8B ($n{=}3$ each), compared to SFT cross-seed variation of 2.79pp, 3.80pp, and 3.23pp respectively. While these measure different sources of variance (SFT reflects full training runs from different random seeds; RSFT reflects additional fine-tuning from a fixed base), the practical consequence is direct: within-scale variance compression is 31$\\times$ at 1.7B, 7.5$\\times$ at 4B, and 17$\\times$ at 8B. The 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) is both more accurate and more predictable than the 8B SFT model (50.19, std 3.23pp), achieving +1.0pp higher mean at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters (cross-scale compression 36$\\times$). Cross-family, LLaMA-3.1-8B exhibits complete pairwise seed separation: all RSFT seeds (55.87--57.14) exceed all SFT seeds (48.02--53.84), with Cohen's $d{=}2.58$ and 4.6$\\times$ variance compression (std 2.92pp $\\to$ 0.64pp). The SFT seed lottery is starkest at LLaMA s44 (48.02 Ign-F1, 24.4\\% truncation rate, elevated Evi-F1 85\\%), a degenerate outcome that RSFT eliminates entirely (s44: 55.87). This robustness extends to inference-time format changes: LLaMA SFT models evaluated without evidence at 1024 tokens suffer 38--42\\% truncation with Ign-F1 collapsing to ${\\sim}$0.33 (s43/s44), while RSFT models under identical conditions truncate only 18--19\\% with stable performance (55.41$\\pm$0.13pp), indicating that CED filtering selects for compact output structure.\n\nFor RSFT models, the inference-time evidence tax (same model evaluated with vs.\\ without evidence output) preserves the benefit direction at 1.7B ($-$7.75pp, confirmed with 2048-token no-evidence evaluation that rules out truncation). At 4B, where the training-time tax is approximately neutral (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_tax}), the RSFT inference-time tax is similarly small (+0.94pp). At 8B, the tax reverses to a benefit ($-$0.78pp), and at 14B the benefit is largest ($-$4.54pp). RSFT thus maintains or improves extraction quality with evidence across the Qwen3 family. LLaMA-3.1-8B RSFT shows a larger inference-time evidence benefit ($-$2.17$\\pm$0.76pp, three-seed mean, vs.\\ $-$0.78pp for Qwen3-8B), confirming that both performance gains and training reliability generalize across model families.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across model scales (DocRED dev Ign-F1). LLaMA-3.1-8B included for cross-family validation. Mean $\\pm$ std$^{(\\text{seeds})}$.}\n\\label{tab:scaling}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{SFT w/ evi} & \\textbf{RSFT} & \\textbf{RSFT Tax}$^\\dagger$ & \\textbf{RSFT Gain} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-1.7B & 44.64{\\scriptsize$\\pm$2.79}$^{(3)}$ & 51.19{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.09}$^{(3)}$ & $-$7.75 & +6.55 \\\\\nQwen3-4B & 43.58{\\scriptsize$\\pm$3.80}$^{(3)}$ & 44.82{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.51}$^{(3)}$ & +0.94 & +1.24 \\\\\nQwen3-8B & 50.19{\\scriptsize$\\pm$3.23}$^{(3)}$ & 54.52{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.19}$^{(3)}$ & $-$0.78 & +4.33 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & 55.07 & 58.86 & $-$4.54 & +3.79 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & 51.09{\\scriptsize$\\pm$2.92}$^{(3)}$ & 56.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.64}$^{(3)}$ & $-$2.17{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.76}$^{(3)}$ & +5.45 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Inference-time: same RSFT model with vs.\\ without evidence output. No-evi eval uses \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}$=$2048; with-evi eval uses 1024 (1.7B uses 2048 for both). Tax $=$ no-evi $-$ with-evi; positive $=$ cost. Seeds: 1.7B s42; 4B Method B s44; 8B, 14B: s42; LLaMA: three-seed mean. Training-time tax (matched@2048 fair evaluation) in Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_tax}.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Evidence Strategy Comparison}\n\\label{sec:evidence_strategy}\n\nWe compare joint generation against post-hoc BM25 retrieval (entity-constrained, $k{=}2$), using Evi-F1 (joint) with the DREEAM-protocol denominator~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}. Post-hoc retrieval achieves higher Rel-F1 (48.76) by avoiding the evidence tax but lower Evi-F1 (joint) of 34.7 (EDCR 0.766). RSFT CED reaches 36.1 (EDCR 0.654), a 1.4-point evidence advantage. CED reranking at inference (best-of-$N{=}8$ by CED score) achieves Rel-F1 48.64 and Evi-F1 81.88 (TP-only), approaching no-evidence extraction quality while preserving evidence.\n\n\n\\subsection{Generalization}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nCross-architecture validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B (Table~\\ref{tab:scaling}) shows the largest RSFT gain (+5.45pp) with complete pairwise seed separation (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). The RSFT improvement transfers across datasets: on Re-DocRED~\\citep{tan2022redocred}, RSFT achieves 41.45 Ign-F1 (+4.86pp over SFT 36.59), matching the DocRED gain (+5.08pp). Our best joint Evi-F1 (36.1, DREEAM-protocol) falls below supervised DREEAM (54.01\\%~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}), a gap driven by extraction performance rather than evidence quality (TP-only Evi-F1 exceeds 80\\%). On the Re-DocRED test set, RSFT Ign-F1 drops approximately 20pp relative to dev across all evaluated scales (e.g., 14B: 58.9$\\to$38.5), attributable to 2.82$\\times$ higher annotation density in test (34.7 vs.\\ 12.3 gold triples per document); model ranking is perfectly preserved (Spearman $\\rho{=}1.0$; Table~\\ref{tab:test_results} in Appendix). Full results appear in Appendix~\\ref{sec:generalization_full}.\n",

      }

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.
4	Recent approaches based on Large Language Models (LLMs) have closed this gap: \citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.
5	These methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.
6	Yet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.
7	In deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.
8	
9	The absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.
10	Relation extraction outputs feed downstream knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows; when a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.
11	Encoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.
12	Both produce evidence annotations and improve extraction quality, but encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy.
13	The community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.
14	
15	This gap reflects a deeper structural difference in how evidence functions across architectures and scales.
16	In encoder-based methods such as SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision; the evidence signal regularizes the encoder and improves extraction at minimal cost.
17	In LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.
18	Yet the role of this generation cost is not fixed.
19	It depends on at least three factors: \emph{model capacity}, where small models appear to benefit from implicit regularization while mid-scale and larger models show approximately neutral impact as capacity absorbs the generation overhead; \emph{optimization paradigm}, where gradient-based methods create destructive trade-offs between evidence and extraction objectives while discrete sample selection avoids them entirely; and \emph{training data quality}, where filtering based on Natural Language Inference (NLI) scores can transform evidence from an optimization burden into a learning scaffold.
20	Understanding this landscape is a prerequisite for deploying evidence-grounded extraction systems that are both accurate and verifiable.
21	
22	We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \citealt{zelikman2022star}).
23	Our experiments span three model scales with full RSFT evaluation (1.7B, 4B, 8B), 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets across 85+ configurations. They reveal that the role of evidence generation is governed by three interacting factors.
24	Along the \emph{capacity dimension}, we observe a within-family capacity threshold (within the Qwen3 family): the evidence tax suggests implicit regularization at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp Ign-F1) but is approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B, +0.04pp at 8B).
25	Along the \emph{paradigm dimension}, we observe a decoupling spectrum in which the strength of optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\rightarrow$ RSFT none); catastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \citealt{hu2022lora}) in GRPO, though full-precision GRPO maintains extraction above the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) baseline with only mild decoupling (EDCR 0.6318 vs.\ SFT 0.6797), which indicates that the collapse is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.
26	Along the \emph{data quality dimension}, RSFT with NLI-based scoring navigates this landscape by selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based pressure; it improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp) while compressing Qwen3 training variance 7.5--17$\times$ and LLaMA variance 4.6$\times$.
27	
28	Our contributions are as follows:
29	\begin{enumerate}
30	    \item We map the \textbf{evidence generation landscape} through a comprehensive empirical study spanning 85+ experiments across three fully evaluated model scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms. We observe a \textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax} with a within-family capacity threshold (Qwen3): beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp) but approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp). This challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.
31	
32	    \item We characterize a \textbf{decoupling spectrum} that explains why gradient-based paradigms fail at joint evidence-and-extraction generation: RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric, validated through per-relation analysis ($\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$, $p < 0.001$).
33	
34	    \item We establish \textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective navigation strategy for this landscape. RSFT improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp) with 7.5--17$\times$ Qwen3 variance compression and 4.6$\times$ on LLaMA (complete pairwise seed separation, Cohen's $d{=}2.58$). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) while being 4.7$\times$ smaller and substantially more predictable, enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction.
35	\end{enumerate}
36	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Kseb6PNWx1fZFdFHYCkiSd`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Task: Evidence-Grounded Document-Level Relation Extraction}
5	\label{sec:task}
6	
7	Given a document $D = \{s_1, \ldots, s_n\}$ consisting of $n$ sentences and a set of entities $\mathcal{E}$ identified within $D$, the task of evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires predicting, for each entity pair $(e_h, e_t) \in \mathcal{E} \times \mathcal{E}$, a relation $r \in \mathcal{R} \cup \{\texttt{NA}\}$ together with a set of supporting evidence sentences $E \subseteq D$. Standard DocRE predicts only the triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$; the evidence-grounded formulation also requires $E$, which grounds each prediction in specific textual support.
8	
9	In our LLM-based approach, the model generates structured outputs that jointly encode both components. For each predicted relation, the output follows the format \texttt{(head, relation, tail) | Evidence: [S$i$][S$j$]}, requiring the model to produce complete evidence references inline with each triple rather than as a separate retrieval step. This design contrasts with encoder-based evidence methods such as SAIS~\citep{xiao2022sais} and DREEAM~\citep{ma2023dreeam}, which frame evidence identification as sentence-level binary classification. Because classification over a fixed sentence set does not increase output length, these methods incur no generation overhead. Our LLM formulation requires additional tokens for each evidence reference, creating a generation burden that scales with the number of predicted triples and their evidence sentences. This scale-dependent cost is the central phenomenon we investigate.
10	
11	
12	\subsection{Supervised Fine-Tuning Baseline}
13	\label{sec:sft}
14	
15	We fine-tune all models with LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} at rank $r{=}64$ and scaling factor $\alpha{=}128$, applied to all linear projections (attention and feed-forward). The training data consists of DocRED~\citep{yao2019docred} training documents converted to the structured output format described above, where each example maps a document and its entity set to the complete set of relation triples with evidence annotations. We also train a \emph{no-evidence} variant under identical configuration but with evidence references removed from the output format, producing only \texttt{(head, relation, tail)}. This variant serves as an upper bound on extraction performance by eliminating the generation cost of evidence, and allows direct measurement of the evidence tax at each model scale. Detailed hyperparameters (learning rate, batch size, number of epochs) are reported in the Appendix.
16	
17	
18	\subsection{Optimization Paradigms}
19	\label{sec:paradigms}
20	
21	We evaluate three post-SFT optimization paradigms that differ in how they incorporate quality signals from the CED scoring function (\S\ref{sec:ced}).
22	
23	\paragraph{GRPO.}
24	Group Relative Policy Optimization~\citep{shao2024deepseekmath} is an online RL method that eliminates the need for a separate value network by computing advantages within groups of sampled outputs. For each training document, the policy generates $G$ candidate responses, which are scored by CED. The advantage of each response is computed relative to the group mean, and the policy is updated to increase the probability of above-average outputs. We test two configurations: QLoRA with $G{=}4$ groups under 4-bit quantization, and bf16 full-precision LoRA with $G{=}8$. Because GRPO applies gradient updates that directly optimize the reward signal, evidence quality and extraction accuracy compete for gradient capacity. We analyze this multi-objective tension in Section~\ref{sec:spectrum}.
25	
26	\paragraph{DPO.}
27	Direct Preference Optimization~\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF as a classification problem over preference pairs, without explicit reward models. We construct preference data from the SFT model's sampled outputs: for each document, the response with the highest CED score is labeled as preferred and the lowest-scoring response as rejected. DPO then adjusts the policy to increase the log-probability ratio between preferred and rejected completions. We train for 1 and 3 epochs to observe the temporal dynamics of evidence-extraction trade-offs under implicit optimization.
28	
29	\paragraph{RSFT.}
30	Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning is a single-round variant of iterative self-improvement methods such as STaR~\citep{zelikman2022star}. The procedure is straightforward: the SFT model generates $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per training document, each candidate is scored by CED, and only the top-$k{=}1$ response is retained as training data. We use $k{=}1$ (best-of-$N$) selection throughout all experiments, selecting the single highest-scoring candidate per document for RSFT training data construction. This yields approximately 3,000 training examples per model (one per document; exact counts vary slightly by model due to generation failures). Unlike GRPO and DPO, RSFT does not apply gradient-based optimization pressure toward a reward signal. Instead, it improves training data quality through filtering: the model trains on its own best outputs rather than all outputs. This distinction is critical because discrete selection over complete outputs cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find. The contribution of RSFT in our setting is not the framework itself, which is well-established, but its application to evidence-grounded DocRE with CED as a domain-specific quality filter.
31	
32	
33	\subsection{Contrastive Evidence Discrimination}
34	\label{sec:ced}
35	
36	CED is a scoring function that evaluates the faithfulness of predicted evidence sentences to their associated relation triples. It builds on Natural Language Inference (NLI) to measure whether evidence actually entails the predicted relation, rather than merely co-occurring with the relevant entities.
37	
38	For a predicted triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$ with evidence sentences $E$, CED constructs a positive NLI pair: the premise is the concatenation of sentences in $E$, and the hypothesis is a natural-language verbalization of the relation (e.g., ``$e_h$ has relation $r$ with $e_t$''). An NLI model (\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}) produces an entailment probability $p^+$ for this pair. The contrastive component introduces hard negatives drawn from the same document. For each triple, we collect sentences that mention $e_h$ or $e_t$ but are not in the predicted evidence set. These entity-sharing distractors serve as negative premises, and the NLI model scores each to obtain $p^-_1, \ldots, p^-_m$. The CED score for a single triple is:
39	\begin{equation}
40	\text{CED}(e_h, r, e_t, E) = p^+ - \max_j \, p^-_j
41	\label{eq:ced}
42	\end{equation}
43	This formulation forces the scoring function to distinguish sentences that genuinely support the relation from sentences that merely mention the same entities. A high CED score indicates that the evidence provides relation-specific support beyond entity co-occurrence.
44	
45	The document-level CED score aggregates over all predicted triples, weighted by extraction correctness: triples that match ground-truth relations contribute their CED scores, while incorrect triples receive zero credit. This coupling ensures that CED rewards both faithful evidence and accurate extraction jointly.
46	
47	We also evaluate a \emph{flat-NLI} baseline that uses only the positive entailment score $p^+$ without the contrastive term. CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (mean Rel-F1 difference of 0.02pp across available seeds; Section~\ref{sec:analysis}), confirming that the contrastive formulation is not essential for data selection quality. We adopt CED as the default because its per-triple discrimination scores support interpretable error analysis.
48	
49	
50	\subsection{Evidence-Decoupling Ratio}
51	\label{sec:edcr}
52	
53	We introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric that quantifies the degree of misalignment between a model's extraction and evidence capabilities. EDCR is not a training component; it serves purely as an analytical tool for comparing optimization methods.
54	
55	EDCR is defined as:
56	\begin{equation}
57	\text{EDCR} = \frac{\text{Evi-Recall}_{\text{TP}}}{\text{Rel-Recall}}
58	\label{eq:edcr}
59	\end{equation}
60	where $\text{Evi-Recall}_{\text{TP}}$ is evidence sentence recall computed only over correctly predicted triples (true positives), and $\text{Rel-Recall}$ is overall relation extraction recall. The numerator captures evidence quality on the subset of triples the model gets right; the denominator captures how many triples the model identifies in total.
61	
62	The intuition is as follows. A model that correctly identifies a few triples and provides excellent evidence for them, but misses most triples entirely, will have high $\text{Evi-Recall}_{\text{TP}}$ and low $\text{Rel-Recall}$, yielding a high EDCR. This pattern signals that evidence quality has decoupled from extraction capability: the model produces faithful evidence only within a narrow comfort zone.
63	
64	EDCR operates at two diagnostic levels. At the method level, comparing EDCR across seven independent method configurations reveals a negative correlation with Rel-F1 (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.89$, $n{=}7$, $p{=}0.007$). Because EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction, this correlation should be interpreted as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent discovery; its primary value lies in per-relation analysis. We validate EDCR's diagnostic utility through per-relation analysis (\S\ref{sec:analysis}), which avoids the shared-component concern present in cross-method correlation. At the per-relation level, computing EDCR for individual relation types reveals a robust negative correlation with Rel-F1 ($\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$) that is independent of Evi-F1 ($\rho{=}{-}0.06$, $p{=}0.58$). This independence means EDCR captures relation-specific extraction-evidence misalignment that aggregate evidence metrics miss. The SFT baseline EDCR (0.6982) reflects the inherent cost of evidence generation; EDCR values substantially exceeding this level (e.g., GRPO QLoRA at 0.9876) indicate RL-induced decoupling beyond what evidence production alone explains, while values below the baseline (e.g., RSFT at 0.6503) reflect improved co-optimization.
65

Standard DocRE predicts only the triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$; the evidence-grounded formulation also requires $E$, which grounds each prediction in specific textual support.\n\nIn our LLM-based approach, the model generates structured outputs that jointly encode both components. This design contrasts with encoder-based evidence methods such as SAIS~\\citep{xiao2022sais} and DREEAM~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}, which frame evidence identification as sentence-level binary classification. The training data consists of DocRED~\\citep{yao2019docred} training documents converted to the structured output format described above, where each example maps a document and its entity set to the complete set of relation triples with evidence annotations. For each training document, the policy generates $G$ candidate responses, which are scored by CED. The advantage of each response is computed relative to the group mean, and the policy is updated to increase the probability of above-average outputs. We construct preference data from the SFT model's sampled outputs: for each document, the response with the highest CED score is labeled as preferred and the lowest-scoring response as rejected. The procedure is straightforward: the SFT model generates $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per training document, each candidate is scored by CED, and only the top-$k{=}1$ response is retained as training data. Then: "The prevailing assumption is that this generation cost always harms extraction. Our experiments show this is wrong. The cost is not fixed but forms a landscape shaped by three interacting factors: capacity (beneficial at 1.7B, neutral at ≥4B), optimization paradigm (gradient methods create destructive trade-offs, discrete selection avoids them), and data quality (NLI-based filtering transforms evidence from burden to scaffold)."

For the formalization, I'm comparing GRPO, DPO, and RSFT across three scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, testing two model families and two datasets.

The key contributions are: evidence's impact varies with scale—beneficial at 1.7B but neutral at larger sizes, challenging the uniform-cost assumption; a decoupling spectrum with the EDCR diagnostic to measure it; and RSFT's remarkable variance compression of 7.5-31×, where the small model approaches the 8B performance at 4.7× fewer parameters. I should also note that at small scale, evidence generation halves output duplication rates, suggesting an implicit regularization effect that deserves mention in the contributions. 现在我看到问题的关键：虽然最近的大语言模型方法在DocRED和Re-DocRED上接近或超过了编码器基线，但它们都只输出裸关系三元组，没有指出哪些句子支持每个预测。在临床知识库、法律尽职调查或金融合规等实际应用中，用户无法在多页文档中手动定位文本依据来验证预测。

编码器方法已经部分解决了这个需求——有些工作引入了句子级证据监督，还有的通过动态证据检索改进了提取质量——但这些方法仍有局限。

现在我面对的是一个结构性的矛盾：最准确的提取器无法提供证据，而能提供证据的方法在准确性上已经不再有竞争力。

这个差距的根本原因在于，对于编码器方法，证据检索是一种分类任务，成本很低；但对于基于大语言模型的DocRE，证据需要为每个三元组生成额外的输出token。直观上看，这种生成成本应该会损害提取准确性，但我的实验表明情况并非如此——证据生成的代价不是固定的惩罚，而是由模型容量、优化范式和训练数据质量共同塑造的。在Qwen3系列中，证据生成在1.7B参数时实际上*有益于*提取准确性，但在4B及以上参数时基本保持中立。

梯度优化会加剧证据和提取之间的竞争——GRPO在精度约束下会导致提取性能崩溃，DPO则逐步用提取准确性换取证据质量——而离散样本选择方法（RSFT）能够完全避免这种权衡。

我正在形式化证据驱动的DocRE作为一个结构化生成任务，并在三个完整评估的规模（1.7B、4B、8B）、两个模型族和两个数据集上比较三种后训练范式。

现在我在列举主要贡献：首先，证据生成的影响取决于模型规模——在1.7B时有害，但在4B及以上时基本中立，这挑战了证据总是损害提取准确性的假设。其次，梯度压力导致证据和提取的解耦，我引入了证据解耦比（EDCR）作为诊断指标来量化这种现象。

最后，相对自监督微调能显著压缩训练方差，通过NLI样本选择改进性能，使得1.7B模型能以4.7倍更少的参数接近8B模型的表现，这对部署效率很有意义。

现在我需要检查P2段落，看看能否进一步精简那个关于关系抽取输出流向知识图谱构建的句子——它提供了很好的具体例子，但可能过于冗长。

我会保留导师批准的核心内容：从"天真的期望"到"但这是错的"再到"现状"的论证弧，每个贡献点控制在3行以内并以洞察开头，删除"85+实验"的表述。同时我要在1.7B证据税的句子中加入"通过减半输出重复"作为括号内的补充，这样既满足导师要求的重复正则化预告，又不会显得突兀。最后确保引用格式保持一致。

  "old_string": "Document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent approaches based on Large Language Models (LLMs) have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.\nYet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \\emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.\n\nThe absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.\nRelation extraction outputs feed downstream knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows; when a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth produce evidence annotations and improve extraction quality, but encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy.\nThe community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a deeper structural difference in how evidence functions across architectures and scales.\nIn encoder-based methods such as SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision; the evidence signal regularizes the encoder and improves extraction at minimal cost.\nIn LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \\emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nYet the role of this generation cost is not fixed.\nIt depends on at least three factors: \\emph{model capacity}, where small models appear to benefit from implicit regularization while mid-scale and larger models show approximately neutral impact as capacity absorbs the generation overhead; \\emph{optimization paradigm}, where gradient-based methods create destructive trade-offs between evidence and extraction objectives while discrete sample selection avoids them entirely; and \\emph{training data quality}, where filtering based on Natural Language Inference (NLI) scores can transform evidence from an optimization burden into a learning scaffold.\nUnderstanding this landscape is a prerequisite for deploying evidence-grounded extraction systems that are both accurate and verifiable.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star}).\nOur experiments span three model scales with full RSFT evaluation (1.7B, 4B, 8B), 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets across 85+ configurations. They reveal that the role of evidence generation is governed by three interacting factors.\nAlong the \\emph{capacity dimension}, we observe a within-family capacity threshold (within the Qwen3 family): the evidence tax suggests implicit regularization at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp Ign-F1) but is approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B, +0.04pp at 8B).\nAlong the \\emph{paradigm dimension}, we observe a decoupling spectrum in which the strength of optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none); catastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \\citealt{hu2022lora}) in GRPO, though full-precision GRPO maintains extraction above the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) baseline with only mild decoupling (EDCR 0.6318 vs.\\ SFT 0.6797), which indicates that the collapse is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.\nAlong the \\emph{data quality dimension}, RSFT with NLI-based scoring navigates this landscape by selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based pressure; it improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp) while compressing Qwen3 training variance 7.5--17$\\times$ and LLaMA variance 4.6$\\times$.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We map the \\textbf{evidence generation landscape} through a comprehensive empirical study spanning 85+ experiments across three fully evaluated model scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms. We observe a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax} with a within-family capacity threshold (Qwen3): beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp) but approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp). This challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} that explains why gradient-based paradigms fail at joint evidence-and-extraction generation: RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric, validated through per-relation analysis ($\\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$, $p < 0.001$).\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective navigation strategy for this landscape. RSFT improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp) with 7.5--17$\\times$ Qwen3 variance compression and 4.6$\\times$ on LLaMA (complete pairwise seed separation, Cohen's $d{=}2.58$). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) while being 4.7$\\times$ smaller and substantially more predictable, enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction.\n\\end{enumerate}",

in LLM-based DocRE, evidence requires the model to \\emph{generate} additional output tokens per triple, increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nThe na\\\"{i}ve expectation is that this generation cost always harms extraction accuracy.\nOur experiments show this expectation is wrong.\nThe evidence tax is not a fixed penalty but a landscape shaped by model capacity, optimization paradigm, and training data quality.\nWithin the Qwen3 family, evidence generation \\emph{benefits} extraction at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp Ign-F1, partly through halving output duplication) but is approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B).\nGradient-based optimization amplifies this competition---GRPO collapses extraction under constrained precision, DPO progressively trades extraction for evidence---while discrete sample selection (RSFT) avoids the trade-off entirely by filtering outputs rather than pushing gradients.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and compare three post-training paradigms---GRPO \\citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, DPO \\citep{rafailov2023direct}, and RSFT (building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star})---across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. Our contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Evidence generation is not uniformly harmful.} We observe a scale-dependent evidence tax with a within-family capacity threshold: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp) but approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B), challenging the assumption that evidence always costs extraction accuracy.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Gradient pressure causes evidence-extraction decoupling.} We characterize a decoupling spectrum (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none) and introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic, validated at per-relation level ($\\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{RSFT compresses training variance 7.5--31$\\times$.} NLI-based sample selection improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp); our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters, enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction.\n\\end{enumerate}"

          " Document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.",
          "-Recent approaches based on Large Language Models (LLMs) have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.",
          "-These methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.",
          "-Yet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \\emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.",
          "-In deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.",
          "+Recent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.",
          "+These methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy, yet every one outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without indicating \\emph{which sentences} support each prediction.",
          "+In deployment contexts---clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, financial compliance systems---users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.",
          " ",
          "-The absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.",
          "-Relation extraction outputs feed downstream knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows; when a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.",
          " Encoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.",
          "-Both produce evidence annotations and improve extraction quality, but encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy.",
          "-The community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.",
          "+Both produce evidence and improve extraction, but encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in accuracy.",
          "+The community faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.",
          " ",
          "-This gap reflects a deeper structural difference in how evidence functions across architectures and scales.",
          "-In encoder-based methods such as SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision; the evidence signal regularizes the encoder and improves extraction at minimal cost.",
          "-In LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \\emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.",
          "-Yet the role of this generation cost is not fixed.",
          "-It depends on at least three factors: \\emph{model capacity}, where small models appear to benefit from implicit regularization while mid-scale and larger models show approximately neutral impact as capacity absorbs the generation overhead; \\emph{optimization paradigm}, where gradient-based methods create destructive trade-offs between evidence and extraction objectives while discrete sample selection avoids them entirely; and \\emph{training data quality}, where filtering based on Natural Language Inference (NLI) scores can transform evidence from an optimization burden into a learning scaffold.",
          "-Understanding this landscape is a prerequisite for deploying evidence-grounded extraction systems that are both accurate and verifiable.",
          "+This gap reflects a structural difference.",
          "+In encoder methods, evidence retrieval is classification that regularizes the encoder at negligible cost; in LLM-based DocRE, evidence requires the model to \\emph{generate} additional output tokens per triple, increasing output length and decoding complexity.",
          "+The na\\\"{i}ve expectation is that this generation cost always harms extraction accuracy.",
          "+Our experiments show this expectation is wrong.",
          "+The evidence tax is not a fixed penalty but a landscape shaped by model capacity, optimization paradigm, and training data quality.",
          "+Within the Qwen3 family, evidence generation \\emph{benefits} extraction at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp Ign-F1, partly through halving output duplication) but is approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B).",
          "+Gradient-based optimization amplifies this competition---GRPO collapses extraction under constrained precision, DPO progressively trades extraction for evidence---while discrete sample selection (RSFT) avoids the trade-off entirely by filtering outputs rather than pushing gradients.",
          " ",
          "-We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star}).",
          "-Our experiments span three model scales with full RSFT evaluation (1.7B, 4B, 8B), 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets across 85+ configurations. They reveal that the role of evidence generation is governed by three interacting factors.",
          "-Along the \\emph{capacity dimension}, we observe a within-family capacity threshold (within the Qwen3 family): the evidence tax suggests implicit regularization at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp Ign-F1) but is approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B, +0.04pp at 8B).",
          "-Along the \\emph{paradigm dimension}, we observe a decoupling spectrum in which the strength of optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none); catastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \\citealt{hu2022lora}) in GRPO, though full-precision GRPO maintains extraction above the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) baseline with only mild decoupling (EDCR 0.6318 vs.\\ SFT 0.6797), which indicates that the collapse is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.",
          "-Along the \\emph{data quality dimension}, RSFT with NLI-based scoring navigates this landscape by selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based pressure; it improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp) while compressing Qwen3 training variance 7.5--17$\\times$ and LLaMA variance 4.6$\\times$.",
          "-",
          "-Our contributions are as follows:",
          "+We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and compare three post-training paradigms---GRPO \\citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, DPO \\citep{rafailov2023direct}, and RSFT (building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star})---across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. Our contributions:",
          " \\begin{enumerate}",
          "-    \\item We map the \\textbf{evidence generation landscape} through a comprehensive empirical study spanning 85+ experiments across three fully evaluated model scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms. We observe a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax} with a within-family capacity threshold (Qwen3): beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp) but approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp). This challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.",
          "+    \\item \\textbf{Evidence generation is not uniformly harmful.} We observe a scale-dependent evidence tax with a within-family capacity threshold: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp) but approximately neutral at $\\geq$4B (+0.25$\\pm$0.47pp at 4B), challenging the assumption that evidence always costs extraction accuracy.",
          " ",
          "-    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} that explains why gradient-based paradigms fail at joint evidence-and-extraction generation: RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric, validated through per-relation analysis ($\\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$, $p < 0.001$).",
          "+    \\item \\textbf{Gradient pressure causes evidence-extraction decoupling.} We characterize a decoupling spectrum (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none) and introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic, validated at per-relation level ($\\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$).",
          " ",
          "-    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective navigation strategy for this landscape. RSFT improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp) with 7.5--17$\\times$ Qwen3 variance compression and 4.6$\\times$ on LLaMA (complete pairwise seed separation, Cohen's $d{=}2.58$). Our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) while being 4.7$\\times$ smaller and substantially more predictable, enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction.",
          "+    \\item \\textbf{RSFT compresses training variance 7.5--31$\\times$.} NLI-based sample selection improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp); our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters, enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction.",
          " \\end{enumerate}"

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.
4	Recent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition, \citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.
5	These methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy, yet every one outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without indicating \emph{which sentences} support each prediction.
6	In deployment contexts---clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, financial compliance systems---users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.
7	
8	Encoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.
9	Both produce evidence and improve extraction, but encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in accuracy.
10	The community faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.
11	
12	This gap reflects a structural difference.
13	In encoder methods, evidence retrieval is classification that regularizes the encoder at negligible cost; in LLM-based DocRE, evidence requires the model to \emph{generate} additional output tokens per triple, increasing output length and decoding complexity.
14	The na\"{i}ve expectation is that this generation cost always harms extraction accuracy.
15	Our experiments show this expectation is wrong.
16	The evidence tax is not a fixed penalty but a landscape shaped by model capacity, optimization paradigm, and training data quality.
17	Within the Qwen3 family, evidence generation \emph{benefits} extraction at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp Ign-F1, partly through halving output duplication) but is approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B).
18	Gradient-based optimization amplifies this competition---GRPO collapses extraction under constrained precision, DPO progressively trades extraction for evidence---while discrete sample selection (RSFT) avoids the trade-off entirely by filtering outputs rather than pushing gradients.
19	
20	We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and compare three post-training paradigms---GRPO \citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, DPO \citep{rafailov2023direct}, and RSFT (building on STaR; \citealt{zelikman2022star})---across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. Our contributions:
21	\begin{enumerate}
22	    \item \textbf{Evidence generation is not uniformly harmful.} We observe a scale-dependent evidence tax with a within-family capacity threshold: beneficial at 1.7B ($-$5.63pp) but approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B), challenging the assumption that evidence always costs extraction accuracy.
23	
24	    \item \textbf{Gradient pressure causes evidence-extraction decoupling.} We characterize a decoupling spectrum (GRPO catastrophic $\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\rightarrow$ RSFT none) and introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic, validated at per-relation level ($\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$, $p{<}0.001$).
25	
26	    \item \textbf{RSFT compresses training variance 7.5--31$\times$.} NLI-based sample selection improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp); our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters, enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction.
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
```

While these measure different sources of variance (SFT reflects full training runs from different random seeds; RSFT reflects additional fine-tuning from a fixed base), the practical consequence is direct: within-scale variance compression is 31$\\times$ at 1.7B, 7.5$\\times$ at 4B, and 17$\\times$ at 8B. The 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) is both more accurate and more predictable than the 8B SFT model (50.19, std 3.23pp), achieving +1.0pp higher mean at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters (cross-scale compression 36$\\times$). Cross-family, LLaMA-3.1-8B exhibits complete pairwise seed separation: all RSFT seeds (55.87--57.14) exceed all SFT seeds (48.02--53.84), with Cohen's $d{=}2.58$ and 4.6$\\times$ variance compression (std 2.92pp $\\to$ 0.64pp). The SFT seed lottery is starkest at LLaMA s44 (48.02 Ign-F1, 24.4\\% truncation rate, elevated Evi-F1 85\\%), a degenerate outcome that RSFT eliminates entirely (s44: 55.87). This robustness extends to inference-time format changes: LLaMA SFT models evaluated without evidence at 1024 tokens suffer 38--42\\% truncation with Ign-F1 collapsing to ${\\sim}$0.33 (s43/s44), while RSFT models under identical conditions truncate only 18--19\\% with stable performance (55.41$\\pm$0.13pp), indicating that CED filtering selects for compact output structure.",

The 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\\times$ fewer parameters---more accurate in mean and 36$\\times$ more predictable.\n\nCross-family validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms these patterns. ```text
46	
47	\subsection{Evidence-Extraction Decoupling Spectrum}
48	\label{sec:spectrum}
49	
50	Because evidence and extraction share gradient capacity, different optimization paradigms handle this competition in qualitatively different ways. We organize the outcomes along a \emph{decoupling spectrum} from catastrophic collapse to no decoupling, shown in Table~\ref{tab:spectrum} (Figure~\ref{fig:spectrum} in Appendix).
51	
52	\paragraph{GRPO QLoRA: catastrophic collapse.}
53	Under 4-bit quantization ($G{=}4$), GRPO drives Rel-F1 to 1.38 while Evi-F1 rises to 85.04 (EDCR 0.9876): the model produces well-formed evidence but loses extraction capability. This extreme decoupling co-occurs with constrained precision; full-precision GRPO (below) avoids it.
54	
55	\paragraph{GRPO bf16: mild, variable.}
56	Full-precision GRPO ($G{=}8$) avoids catastrophe, with the best checkpoint achieving Rel-F1 45.49, Evi-F1 80.86, and EDCR 0.6318 (below SFT baseline 0.6797). Both sub-tasks improve jointly, though the variable training trajectory requires careful checkpoint selection.
57	
58	\paragraph{DPO: gradual decay.}
59	DPO progressively trades extraction for evidence: Rel-F1 drops from 40.34 (1 epoch) to 34.29 (3 epochs)\footnote{DPO 3-epoch results use an earlier evaluation protocol; included for trend analysis.} while evidence improves (Evi-F1 80.88$\to$84.03, EDCR 0.7139$\to$0.7294).
60	
61	\paragraph{RSFT: no decoupling.}
62	RSFT maintains both capabilities simultaneously (Rel-F1 45.80, Evi-F1 80.96, EDCR 0.6487) because discrete output selection cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find.
63	
64	\begin{table}[t]
65	\centering
66	\caption{Evidence-extraction decoupling across RL paradigms (Qwen3-4B). Severity indicates the degree of extraction collapse relative to evidence improvement.}
67	\label{tab:spectrum}
68	\small
69	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
70	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
71	\toprule
72	\textbf{Paradigm} & \textbf{Config} & \textbf{Rel-F1} & \textbf{Evi-F1} & \textbf{EDCR} & \textbf{Severity} \\
73	\midrule
74	GRPO & QLoRA $G{=}4$ & 1.38 & 85.04 & 0.9876 & Catastrophic \\
75	GRPO & bf16 $G{=}8$ & 45.49 & 80.86 & 0.6318 & Mild \\
76	DPO & bf16 1ep & 40.34 & 80.88 & 0.7139 & Gradual \\
77	DPO & bf16 3ep$^\dagger$ & 34.29 & 84.03 & 0.7294 & Gradual \\
78	\midrule
79	SFT & baseline$^\ddagger$ & 44.39 & 79.61 & 0.6797 & None \\
80	RSFT & CED$^\ddagger$ & 45.80 & 80.96 & 0.6487 & None \\
81	\bottomrule
82	\end{tabular}%
83	}
84	\vspace{-2mm}
85	{\raggedright\footnotesize $^\dagger$Earlier evaluation protocol. $^\ddagger$Three-seed mean. Severity reflects training trajectory stability (propensity for extraction collapse during optimization), not the final-checkpoint EDCR value.\par}
86	\end{table}
87	
88	
89	\subsection{Scale-Dependent Evidence Tax}
90	\label{sec:scaling}
91	
92	The evidence tax, defined as Ign-F1(no-evidence) $-$ Ign-F1(with-evidence) on the development set, is scale-dependent. Under matched evaluation conditions (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}$=$2048, format-compliant intersection; Appendix~\ref{sec:training_tax}), the training-time tax reveals a within-family capacity threshold: evidence generation benefits 1.7B ($-$5.63pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:training_tax}), where it also halves the output duplication rate (60.5\%$\to$33.0\%; Appendix~\ref{sec:error_analysis}), but is approximately neutral at $\geq$4B (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B, $n{=}3$; +0.04pp at 8B; +0.03pp at 14B). Table~\ref{tab:scaling} presents RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across five models (Figure~\ref{fig:scaling} in Appendix).
93	
94	RSFT navigates this capacity landscape through quality-based data selection rather than gradient pressure. Mean Ign-F1 improves across tested scales: +6.55pp at 1.7B, +1.24pp at 4B, +4.33pp at 8B, and +3.79pp at 14B (Table~\ref{tab:scaling}). RSFT from an independent SFT base (s44, Method B) achieves 51.52 Ign-F1 at 4B (+4.18pp over that base). This result confirms that RSFT gains are not contingent on a specific SFT checkpoint.
95	
96	Beyond mean performance, RSFT substantially improves training reliability. Three RSFT seeds from a shared SFT checkpoint yield standard deviations of 0.09pp at 1.7B, 0.51pp at 4B, and 0.19pp at 8B ($n{=}3$ each), compared to SFT cross-seed variation of 2.79pp, 3.80pp, and 3.23pp respectively. While these measure different sources of variance (SFT reflects full training runs from different random seeds; RSFT reflects additional fine-tuning from a fixed base), the practical consequence is direct: variance compression is 31$\times$ at 1.7B, 7.5$\times$ at 4B, and 17$\times$ at 8B. The 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7$\times$ fewer parameters---more accurate in mean and 36$\times$ more predictable.
97	
98	Cross-family validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms these patterns. All three RSFT seeds (55.87--57.14) exceed all three SFT seeds (48.02--53.84), yielding complete pairwise seed separation (Cohen's $d{=}2.58$) and 4.6$\times$ variance compression (std 2.92pp $\to$ 0.64pp). The SFT seed lottery is starkest at s44 (48.02 Ign-F1, 24.4\% truncation, elevated Evi-F1 85\%), a degenerate outcome that RSFT eliminates entirely (s44: 55.87). This robustness extends to inference-time format changes: LLaMA SFT models evaluated without evidence at 1024 tokens suffer 38--42\% truncation with Ign-F1 collapsing to ${\sim}$0.33 (s43/s44), while RSFT models truncate only 18--19\% with stable performance (55.41$\pm$0.13pp), indicating that CED filtering selects for compact output structure.
99	
100	For RSFT models, the inference-time evidence tax (same model evaluated with vs.\ without evidence output) preserves the benefit direction at 1.7B ($-$7.75pp, confirmed with 2048-token no-evidence evaluation that rules out truncation). At 4B, where the training-time tax is approximately neutral (+0.25$\pm$0.47pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:training_tax}), the RSFT inference-time tax is similarly small (+0.94pp). At 8B, the tax reverses to a benefit ($-$0.78pp), and at 14B the benefit is largest ($-$4.54pp). RSFT thus maintains or improves extraction quality with evidence across the Qwen3 family. LLaMA-3.1-8B RSFT shows a larger inference-time evidence benefit ($-$2.17$\pm$0.76pp, three-seed mean, vs.\ $-$0.78pp for Qwen3-8B), confirming that both performance gains and training reliability generalize across model families.
101	
102	\begin{table}[t]
103	\centering
104	\caption{RSFT performance and inference-time evidence tax across model scales (DocRED dev Ign-F1). LLaMA-3.1-8B included for cross-family validation. Mean $\pm$ std$^{(\text{seeds})}$.}
105	\label{tab:scaling}
```

_source line 7632 · 2026-05-22T07:28:17.878Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
Current conclusion (from earlier read):

```
\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

We formalize evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction as a structured generation task and systematically compare three post-training paradigms across three fully evaluated scales (1.7B, 4B, 8B) with 14B validation, two model families, and two datasets. Our experiments reveal that evidence generation plays a scale-dependent role shaped by three interacting factors: model capacity appears to determine whether evidence acts as regularizer ($-$5.63pp at 1.7B) or is approximately neutral ($\geq$4B; +0.25$\pm$0.47pp at 4B) within the Qwen3 family, optimization paradigm determines decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic under QLoRA, DPO gradual, RSFT none), and data quality filtering through NLI-based scoring enables RSFT to navigate this landscape without gradient-induced trade-offs.

RSFT improves mean Ign-F1 across tested scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp) while compressing Qwen3 training variance 7.5--17× and LLaMA variance 4.6× (complete pairwise seed separation, d=2.58). The resulting 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1, std 0.09pp) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19, std 3.23pp) at 4.7× fewer parameters, providing both higher mean accuracy and substantially greater training reliability for deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction.
```

I'm seeing the feedback about splitting this into multiple paragraphs and avoiding number repetition. Let me restructure this to be more narrative-driven while keeping the key findings distinct.

The main point here is that RSFT achieves significant variance reduction across model scales through output filtering rather than gradient-based optimization, and this efficiency gain means smaller models can match much larger ones. Then I should briefly note what remains unexplored—particularly whether we can push evidence quality further with targeted rewards, and how this approach generalizes beyond Wikipedia where evidence is harder to annotate. I'm adding back the scope statement to ground the conclusion, then wrapping up with the key findings about variance compression and parameter efficiency without repeating the exact numbers I already highlighted. The consistency checks out—the abstract and conclusion align on the key findings around capacity, paradigm, and data quality, with the performance metrics matching across both sections.
````

</details>

    "newString": "Primary development is on the DocRED development set; Re-DocRED test-set evaluation preserves model ranking (Spearman $\\rho{=}1.0$) but shows a 18.9--21.9pp absolute drop attributable to higher annotation density (2.82$\\times$ more gold triples per document). Exact-match F1 penalizes surface-form-different entity mentions~\\citep{wan2024genres}, likely underestimating absolute extraction capability of generative models, though relative comparisons between methods remain valid.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Limitations}\n\nAll results use the DocRED development set; test set submission is pending. Exact-match F1 penalizes surface-form-different entity mentions~\\citep{wan2024genres}, likely underestimating absolute extraction capability of generative models, though relative comparisons between methods remain valid.\n\nWe evaluate on two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), both derived from Wikipedia. Cross-domain evaluation is constrained by the absence of evidence-annotated DocRE datasets beyond Wikipedia; this limitation is shared by all prior evidence-aware DocRE work (SAIS, DREEAM, EIDER).\n\nWe evaluate one algorithm per RL family (GRPO, DPO) with single hyperparameter settings (KL coefficient 0.01, $\\beta$ 0.1); other algorithms or settings may produce different decoupling behaviors. CED scoring depends on an external NLI model, and the RSFT ablation uses limited seeds (four for CED, two for flat-NLI). Evidence tax patterns span two model families (Qwen3, LLaMA) but may not generalize to other architectures or scales beyond 14B.\n\nEDCR correlates with extraction quality at both method level ($\\rho{=}{-}0.89$, $n{=}7$) and per-relation level ($\\rho{=}{-}0.79$, $n{=}76$), but has not been validated against human judgments of evidence-extraction alignment. Future work should assess whether EDCR rankings correspond to practitioner assessments of output quality.\n\nRSFT improves extraction across scales but evidence quality is unchanged (Evi-F1 $\\Delta < 0.4$pp at all scales; Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_analysis}). CED-based selection filters for joint quality but does not specifically optimize evidence grounding, suggesting that targeted evidence rewards may be needed to improve evidence quality beyond what data selection achieves.\n\nAt 1.7B scale, 44.4\\% of outputs exhibit repetitive patterns (vs.\\ negligible at 4B+); small models may require decoding-time interventions (e.g., repetition penalty) for reliable deployment.\n\n\\section{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmarks (DocRED, Re-DocRED) derived from Wikipedia, containing no private or sensitive information. All models are trained on existing annotated data without human subjects involvement. Our evidence-grounding approach aims to improve the verifiability of relation extraction outputs, which supports responsible deployment in high-stakes domains such as clinical and legal information systems by enabling human verification of model predictions. We release code and model outputs to support reproducibility.\n\n% Responsible NLP Research Checklist: submitted via OpenReview form per ARR/EMNLP 2026 guidelines.\n",

          " We conduct experiments on DocRED~\\citep{yao2019docred}, a large-scale document-level relation extraction benchmark containing 3,053 training and 998 development documents spanning 96 relation types.           " \\paragraph{Training.}",
          " All models use LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} with rank $r{=}64$ and $\\alpha{=}128$ over a structured output format that jointly produces relation triples and evidence sentences. For RSFT, we sample $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per document and select the top-$k{=}1$ response ranked by CED scoring (\\S\\ref{sec:ced}), which combines NLI-based evidence faithfulness with extraction accuracy. GRPO experiments use group sizes $G{=}4$ (QLoRA, 4-bit) and $G{=}8$ (bf16 full-precision LoRA). DPO constructs preference pairs from CED-ranked outputs."

The conclusion is tightened to three focused paragraphs with minimal numerical detail.
